\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}\|\leq C
under Assumption
5
, we can deduce for the
smallest singular value that

\sigma_{\min}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}+(\alpha/z+1)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}}}\right)\geq\frac{|\alpha|}{|z|}-1-|\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}}}}\right\|\geq c

(D.22)

on the event
\{|\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})|\leq C_{0}\}
,
for any
z\in U(\varepsilon)
,
|\alpha|\geq\alpha_{0}
, and some
\alpha_{0},c>0

depending on
\varepsilon,C_{0}
.
Thus also

\mathbf{1}\{\|(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\|>C\}\prec 0

(D.23)

for a constant
C>0
, showing all statements of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Lemma
24
(b).

Recalling the form of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)
in (
D.2
),
the quantity we wish to approximate is

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{v}_{2}=\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{K}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}.

Analogous to (
C.29
) in the proof of Lemma
18
,
for any matrix
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{B}=\operatorname{Tr}(\boldsymbol{K}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}={-}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}}{1+N^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{g}_{i}}.

(D.24)

Applying the definition

m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})=N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})+(1-\gamma_{N})(-1/z)

and the identity (
D.24
) with
\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{I}
, we obtain analogously to
(
C.30
) that

\displaystyle m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma_{N})(-1/z)+\frac{1}{Nz}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-\boldsymbol{I})\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})

\displaystyle={-}\frac{1}{Nz}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{1+N^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{g}_{i}}-\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-\boldsymbol{I})\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}).

Then, noting that
z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-\boldsymbol{I}
has rank
r
and hence

|N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-\boldsymbol{I})\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})|\leq\frac{r}{N}\frac{|\alpha|}{|z|}\|\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\|\prec N^{-1}
, this gives

m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})={-}\frac{1}{Nz}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{1+N^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{g}_{i}}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1}}}\right).

(D.25)

Fixing the unit vectors
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
let us now choose
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}
and

\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}

in (
D.24
), and define

\displaystyle d_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\,\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\,\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}.

Then, combining (
D.24
) and (
D.25
), we get

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}=\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{B}

\displaystyle={-}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-zm_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1}}}\right)}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{d_{i}}{1+N^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{g}_{i}}

\displaystyle={-}z\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{d_{i}}{1+N^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{g}_{i}}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1}}}\right),

(D.26)

where the last equality applies the definition of
\boldsymbol{B}
to combine the first two
terms, and applies also

|\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\leq\|(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\|\prec 1
by Lemma
27
to obtain the

O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1}}}\right)
remainder.

Considering a similar decomposition as in Lemma
18
, we define

d_{i}=d_{i,1}+d_{i,2}+d_{i,3}+d_{i,4}
where

\displaystyle d_{i,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i},

(D.27)

\displaystyle d_{i,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}-\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1},

\displaystyle d_{i,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}-\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1},

\displaystyle d_{i,4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}-\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}(z^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}.

For
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}
, by the bound

\mathbf{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{R}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\|>C_{0}\}\prec 0

from Lemma
27
, we have for a constant
C>0
that

\mathbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{R}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})}}}\right\|_{F}>C\sqrt{N}}}\right\}\prec 0,\quad\mathbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{R}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{A}}}}\right\|_{F}>C}}\right\}\prec 0

(D.28)

uniformly over
z\in U(\varepsilon)
.
Then, employing Lemma
27
and the same bounds as
(
C.35
)–(
C.38
) from the proof of Lemma
22

(where here, the bounds for
\|\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}\boldsymbol{A}\|_{F},\|\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{A}\|_{F}
are
improved by a factor of
N^{-1/2}
because
\boldsymbol{A}
is low-rank),
we conclude that
|d_{i,1}|,|d_{i,3}|,|d_{i,4}|\prec N^{-3/2}
and
|d_{i,2}|\prec N^{-1}
.
Hence, applying also
1+N^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{g}_{i}=1+N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)
as follows from
(
D.19
) and the bound (
C.33
),

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{d_{i}}{1+N^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{g}_{i}}=\frac{1}{1+N^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N}d_{i,2}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right).

By Lemma
13
applied with

\Psi_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})=C\sqrt{N}
and
\Phi_{N}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\boldsymbol{A})=C
for a constant
C>0
,
we have
|\sum_{i}d_{i,2}|\prec N^{-1/2}
. Thus the above quantity is of size

O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)
, so applying this back to (
D.26
),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+zm_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{R}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\prec N^{-1/2}.

Finally, from (
D.16
) and Lemma
26

we have
m_{\tilde{}\boldsymbol{K}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})=\tilde{m}_{N,0}(z)+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1}}}\right)
, and applying
this above completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
25
.

The proof is similar to Lemma
24
, replacing

r
and
n
throughout by
r+1
and
n+1
,

\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(S)}
by
[\boldsymbol{u}^{(S)},\boldsymbol{G}_{r}^{(S)}]
,

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
by
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, and

\boldsymbol{R}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
and
\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
by

\boldsymbol{R}^{(S)}(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})=\big([\boldsymbol{u}^{(S)},\boldsymbol{G}^{(S)}]^{\top}[\boldsymbol{u}^{(S)},\boldsymbol{G}^{(S)}]-\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\big)^{-1},\quad\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{R}^{(S)}(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})+\Big(1-\frac{n+1}{N}\Big)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-}\frac{1}{z}}}\right).

The only difference here is that
\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is no longer
diagonal, leading to the following minor modifications of the preceding
proof: The bound

\frac{1}{n+1}\operatorname{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\end{pmatrix}}}\right)\boldsymbol{R}^{(S)}(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\prec\frac{1}{N}

analogous to (
D.18
) follows upon noting that
(with
\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{r}]^{\top}&\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{0}]^{\top}\end{pmatrix}
)

\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}[u^{2}]&\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{r}]^{\top}&\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{0}]^{\top}\\
\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{r}]&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{0}]&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}

still is of low rank, with rank at most
r+2
.
Writing as shorthand
\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}=\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}(\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma})
, the bound

\mathbf{1}\{\|(z^{-1}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}\|>C_{0}\}\prec 0

analogous to (
D.23
) follows from

(z^{-1}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1}=~\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u^{2}]+\frac{\alpha}{z}+1&\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{r}]^{\top}&\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{0}]^{\top}\\
\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{r}]&\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}+(\frac{\alpha}{z}+1)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}&\mathbf{0}\\
\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{0}]&\mathbf{0}&\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}+\boldsymbol{I}\end{pmatrix}^{-1},

the bound
\mathbf{1}\{\|\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}+\boldsymbol{I}\|^{-1}>C\}\prec 0
for
the lower-right block as follows from
(
D.21
), and the bound for the inverse of
its Schur-complement

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}\Bigg\{\Bigg\|\Bigg[\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u^{2}]+\frac{\alpha}{z}+1&\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{r}]^{\top}\\
\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{r}]&\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}+(\frac{\alpha}{z}+1)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{0}]^{\top}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}(\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}+\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{m}_{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(S)}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}_{0}]&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\Bigg]^{-1}\Bigg\|>C\Bigg\}\prec 0

which holds uniformly over
z\in U(\varepsilon)
for any
|\alpha|>\alpha_{0}

sufficiently large, by an argument analogous to (
D.22
).
The remainder of the proof
is identical to that of Lemma
24
, and we omit the
details.
∎

D.3
Analysis of outliers

Let
\boldsymbol{V}_{r},\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(z,\alpha),\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha),\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}(z,\alpha)
be as defined in the preceding section.
Consider the decomposition of

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}(z,\alpha)
as in (
D.5
)
into its blocks of dimensions 1,
n
, and
N
, and define

\displaystyle\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}(z,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{-z-\alpha+\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(z,\alpha))^{-1}\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}},

(D.29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}(z,\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
0\end{pmatrix}={-}\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}(z,\alpha)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}\Big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(z,\alpha)\Big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{r},

(D.30)

where the second equalities follow from block matrix inversion of
the lower
2\times 2
blocks of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}(z,\alpha)
,
followed by block matrix inversion of the full matrix
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}(z,\alpha)
. Set

\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\alpha\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}.

(D.31)

Proposition 28
.

Fix any
\varepsilon>0
and any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
sufficiently large that satisfies
Lemmas
24
and
25
.
Then on the event
\mathcal{E}\cap\widetilde{}\mathcal{E}
of
Lemmas
24
and
25
,
for all sufficiently large
N
,

(a)

\widehat{\lambda}\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
is an eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}
if and only if

\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)=0
, and its multiplicity as an
eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}
equals the dimension of

\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
.

(b)

Let
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a unit eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}
(i.e. right singular vector of
\boldsymbol{G}
)
corresponding to an eigenvalue
\widehat{\lambda}\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
. Then

\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}
is a non-zero vector in
\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
, and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{n}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}

(D.32)

For any vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}+\alpha\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}=0.

(D.33)

(c)

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be as in Theorem
12
(c), and let
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be
a unit eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{G}\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}
(i.e. left singular vector of
\boldsymbol{G}
)
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\widehat{\lambda}\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
. Then

\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{\alpha}{\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\,\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}.

(D.34)

Proof.

For part (a), note that if
\widehat{\lambda}
is an eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}
,
i.e.
\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}
is a singular value of
\boldsymbol{G}
with left and
right unit singular vectors
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}
and
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}
, then

0=\begin{pmatrix}-\widehat{\lambda}\boldsymbol{I}_{n}&\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\\
\boldsymbol{G}&-\boldsymbol{I}_{N}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}\end{pmatrix}

which implies, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,

{-}\alpha\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}=\begin{pmatrix}-\widehat{\lambda}\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\alpha\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}&\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\\
\boldsymbol{G}&-\boldsymbol{I}_{N}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}\end{pmatrix}.

Fixing
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
large enough, on the event
\mathcal{E}

of Lemma
24
, the generalized resolvent

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)=\begin{pmatrix}-\widehat{\lambda}\boldsymbol{I}_{n}-\alpha\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}&\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\\
\boldsymbol{G}&-\boldsymbol{I}_{N}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}

exists, and multiplying both sides by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
gives

\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}\end{pmatrix}=-\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}.

(D.35)

Then, multiplying by
(\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\;\mathbf{0})
on both sides and re-arranging, we get

\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}=0
.

We remark
that if
\widehat{\lambda}
is
an eigenvalue of multiplicity
k
, and
\boldsymbol{G}
has corresponding linearly
independent left singular vectors
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}

and right singular vectors
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
, then the
vectors
\{(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{j},\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\}_{j=1}^{k}
on the left side
of (
D.35
) are linearly independent, implying that the vectors

\{\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
on the right side must also be
(non-zero and) linearly independent vectors in
\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
.
Conversely, if
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
are linearly independent vectors in

\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
, then defining

\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\\
\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{j}\end{pmatrix}=-\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}_{j}

and multiplying by
(\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\;\mathbf{0})
, we must have

\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}=({-}\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)+\boldsymbol{I})\boldsymbol{y}_{j}=\boldsymbol{y}_{j}
. Thus the
pairs
(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{j},\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})
are linearly independent vectors
satisfying (
D.35
), and multiplying by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)^{-1}
and rearranging shows that

\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}
must be a singular
value of
\boldsymbol{G}
with multiplicity at least
k
, with corresponding
singular vectors
\{(\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{j},\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\}_{j=1}^{k}
.
This establishes part (a).

For part (b), the above argument has shown
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}\in\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
. Multiplying
(
D.35
) on the left by

\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{n}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}

and taking the squared norm (noting that
\widehat{\lambda},\alpha

and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
here are real) shows (
D.32
).
Multiplying (
D.35
) on the left by
(\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\;\mathbf{0})
shows
(
D.33
). For part (c),
multiplying (
D.35
) by
(\mathbf{0}\;\boldsymbol{u}^{\top})
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\,\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\boldsymbol{u}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}=-\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}\Big(\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)\Big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}

where the second equality follows from the block matrix inversion of

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)
.
Then, recalling the forms of
\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}
from (
D.29
) and (
D.30
),
this gives

\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{\alpha\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)}{\widehat{\lambda}^{1/2}\,\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}(\widehat{\lambda},\alpha)}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}

which is (
D.34
).
∎

For notational convenience, let us now introduce the shorthand

\psi_{N,0}(z)=z\tilde{m}_{N,0}(z),\qquad\psi(z)=z\tilde{m}(z).

By Lemma
24
(b) applied with
(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{2})
being the
columns of
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}
,
we see that
\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)
is well-approximated by the (deterministic,

N
-dependent) matrix

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha):=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}-\alpha\Big((\alpha+z)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diag}(\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}),\ldots,\lambda_{r}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))\Big)^{-1}.

(D.36)

To show Theorem
12
(a), we translate this approximation into a
comparison of the roots of
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)
and
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)
,
where the latter are explicitly given by
z_{N,0}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))
for
the function
z_{N,0}(\cdot)
defined in (
B.5
).

Proof of Theorem
12
(a).

Let us fix any
\varepsilon>0
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying Lemmas

24
and
25
, and denote

f_{N,i}(z,\alpha)=1-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+z+\psi_{N,0}(z)\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}

for each
i\in[r]
.
Then
\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{N,i}(z,\alpha)
. Define also the
limiting functions

f_{i}(z,\alpha)=1-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+z+\psi(z)\lambda_{i}},\qquad\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}-\alpha\Big((\alpha+z)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi(z)\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})\Big)^{-1}

so
\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{i}(z,\alpha)
.
We first analyze the roots of
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)
: By the
definition
\psi(z)=z\tilde{m}(z)
, observe that
z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathrm{supp}({\tilde{\mu}})
satisfies
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)
if and only if
either
z=0
or

\tilde{m}(z)=-1/\lambda_{i}\text{ for some }i\in[r].

(This condition is the same for any non-zero
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.)
Let
\mathcal{T}=\{0\}\cup\{-1/\lambda:\lambda\in\mathrm{supp}({\nu})\}
be as in
(
A.3
) where
\nu
is the limit spectral law of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}
.
Then
-1/\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
for all
i\in[r]
under Assumption
6
, so
Proposition
8
implies that
\tilde{m}(z)=-1/\lambda_{i}
holds
for some
z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathrm{supp}({\tilde{\mu}})
if and only if

z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i})>0
, i.e.
i\in\mathcal{I}
. If
i\in\mathcal{I}
, then

\tilde{m}(z)=-1/\lambda_{i}
holds for
z=z(-1/\lambda_{i})
,
and we must have
z(-1/\lambda_{i})>0
strictly because for any
z\leq 0
,
we have
\tilde{m}(z)>0

(and hence
\tilde{m}(z)\neq-1/\lambda_{i}
)
by the definition
\tilde{m}(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}d\tilde{\mu}(x)
.
Thus the roots of
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)
in
\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathrm{supp}({\tilde{\mu}})\cup\{0\})
— and hence
in
U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
— are given
precisely by

z_{i}:=z(-1/\lambda_{i})\text{ for }i\in\mathcal{I}.

Since
\tilde{m}^{\prime}(z)=\int\frac{1}{(x-z)^{2}}d\tilde{\mu}(x)>0

for all
z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{S}
, and
\{\lambda_{i}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
are
distinct by assumption, these values
\{z_{i}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
are simple roots of

0=\det\boldsymbol{M}(z_{i},\alpha)
. Then

(\det\boldsymbol{M})^{\prime}(z_{i},\alpha)\neq 0
where
(\det\boldsymbol{M})^{\prime}
denotes the derivative in

z
.

Lemma
23
(c) implies

\tilde{m}_{N,0}(z)\to\tilde{m}(z)
and
\tilde{m}_{N,0}^{\prime}(z)\to\tilde{m}^{\prime}(z)

uniformly over
z\in U(\varepsilon)
. Since also
\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\to\lambda_{i}
,
we have
\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)\to\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)
and

(\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)\to(\det\boldsymbol{M})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)
uniformly over

z\in U(\varepsilon)
. This, together with the above condition

(\det\boldsymbol{M})^{\prime}(z_{i},\alpha)\neq 0
, imply
that for all large
N
, the roots
z_{N,i}\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}

of
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)

are in 1-to-1 correspondence with, and converge to, the above roots
z_{i}\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
of

0=\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)
. We note that
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)
if and only if
either
z=0
or

\tilde{m}_{N,0}(z)=-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\text{ for some }i\in[r].

(D.37)

For each
i\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\to\lambda_{i}
where

z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i})>0
. Recall from Lemma
23
(a)
that
z_{N,0}(\tilde{m})\to z(\tilde{m})
and
z_{N,0}^{\prime}(\tilde{m})\to z^{\prime}(\tilde{m})

uniformly over compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
.
Then
z_{N,0}^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))\to z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i})
, so
also
z_{N,0}^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))>0
for all large
N
. Then
Proposition
8
implies that (
D.37
) holds
for
z_{N,i}:=z_{N,0}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))
. We have

z_{N,i}\to z_{i}=z(-1/\lambda_{i})
, so these must be the
roots of
\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)
in
U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
. Thus we have shown that
for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and all large
N
,
the roots
z\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
of
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)
are precisely
the values

z_{N,i}:=z_{N,0}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))\text{ for }i\in\mathcal{I},

and
z_{N,i}\to z_{i}>0
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
.

Finally, we apply Lemma
24
(b) with
(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{2})

being the columns of
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}
. On the event
\mathcal{E}
of
Lemma
24
(a), we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)}}}\right\|\leq C,\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)-\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z^{\prime},\alpha)}}}\right\|\leq C|z-z^{\prime}|

(D.38)

for some
C>0
and all
z,z^{\prime}\in U(\varepsilon/2)
.
Also
|\tilde{m}_{N,0}(z)|,|\tilde{m}_{N,0}^{\prime}(z)|<C

for a constant
C>0
, all
z\in U(\varepsilon)
, and all large
N
, and thus

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)}}}\right\|\leq C,\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)-\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z^{\prime},\alpha)}}}\right\|\leq C|z-z^{\prime}|

(D.39)

for some
C>0
and all
z,z^{\prime}\in U(\varepsilon/2)
.
Then, applying Lemma
24
(b) and the Lipschitz
bounds of (
D.38
) and (
D.39
) to take
a union bound over a sufficiently fine covering net of
U(\varepsilon/2)
, we get

\sup_{z\in U(\varepsilon/2)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)-\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)}}}\right\|\prec 1/\sqrt{N}.

(D.40)

Applying also the first bounds of (
D.38
)
and (
D.39
), this gives

\sup_{z\in U(\varepsilon/2)}|\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)-\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)|\prec 1/\sqrt{N}.

(D.41)

Since
\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)
and
\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)
are both holomorphic
over
z\in U(\varepsilon/2)
on this event
\mathcal{E}
,
the Cauchy integral formula then implies

\sup_{z\in U(\varepsilon)}|(\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)-(\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)|\prec 1/\sqrt{N}.

In particular, combining with the uniform convergence statements

\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)\to\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)
and
(\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)\to(\det\boldsymbol{M})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)
over
z\in U(\varepsilon)
as argued above,
this shows that on an event
\mathcal{E}
satisfying
\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{E}^{c}\}\prec 0
and for some
\delta_{N}\to 0
, we have

\sup_{z\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}}|\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)-\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)|,|(\det\boldsymbol{M})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)-(\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)|<\delta_{N}.

Thus, on this event
\mathcal{E}
and as
N\to\infty
, the roots
\widehat{\lambda}_{i}\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
of
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)
are also in 1-to-1 correspondence with, and converge to,
the roots
z_{i}\in U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
of
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}(z,\alpha)
.
Furthermore, the condition
(\det\boldsymbol{M})^{\prime}(z_{i},\alpha)\neq 0
implies that

|(\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)|
and
|(\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}})^{\prime}(z,\alpha)|
are bounded away
from 0 in a neighborhood of each such root
z_{i}
, so (
D.41
)
then implies that the corresponding roots
\widehat{\lambda}_{i}
and
z_{N,i}
of

0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(z,\alpha)
and
0=\det\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)
satisfy

|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{N,i}|\prec 1/\sqrt{N}.

Proposition
28
shows that on this event
\mathcal{E}
,
these roots
\{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
are
precisely the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}
in
U(\varepsilon)\cap\mathbb{R}
.
By the definition of
\boldsymbol{M}(z_{i},\alpha)
,
each root
z_{i}
of
\det\boldsymbol{M}(z_{i},\alpha)
is such that
\ker\boldsymbol{M}(z_{i},\alpha)

has dimension 1. Since
\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{i})\to 0
, we have

\mathbf{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}\|\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{M}(z_{i},\alpha)\|\to 0
,
so
\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)
also has dimension 1 on this
event
\mathcal{E}
for all large
N
. Then Proposition
28
implies that
the eigenvalues
\{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
of
\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}
are
simple, and thus in 1-to-1 correspondence with
\{\lambda_{i}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
.
This proves part (a) of the theorem.

∎

Lemma 29
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem

12
, for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such
that fixing any
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\alpha|>\alpha_{0}
,
uniformly over
z\in U(\varepsilon)
,

\Bigg\|\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{n}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\\
-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\alpha+z)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}}}\right)^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}}}\right)\Bigg\|\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

Proof.

Fix any
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying Lemma
24
, and
denote

f_{N}(z,\alpha):=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix},\qquad g_{N}(z,\alpha):=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\alpha+z)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}}}\right)^{-1}.

Applying Lemma
24
(b) and
the Lipschitz continuity statements of
(
D.38
) and (
D.39
) to take a union bound over a
sufficiently fine covering net of
U(\varepsilon/2)
, we have

\sup_{z\in U(\varepsilon/2)}\|f_{N}(z,\alpha)-g_{N}(z,\alpha)\|\prec 1/\sqrt{N}.

Then by the Cauchy integral formula,

\sup_{z\in U(\varepsilon)}\|f_{N}^{\prime}(z,\alpha)-g_{N}^{\prime}(z,\alpha)\|\prec 1/\sqrt{N}

where
f_{N}^{\prime}
and
g_{N}^{\prime}
denote the entrywise derivatives in
z
.
The lemma follows, since differentiating
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)
in
(
D.2
) shows

f_{N}^{\prime}(z,\alpha)=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z,\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0},\end{pmatrix}

while
g_{N}^{\prime}(z,\alpha)=((\alpha+z)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r})^{-2}(\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r})
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
12
(b).

Let
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
be the given unit-norm eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{K}
with eigenvalue

\widehat{\lambda}_{i}
. Let
z_{N,i}=z_{N,0}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))
and

z_{i}=z(-1/\lambda_{i})
.
Then, fixing any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
large enough to satisfy Lemmas

24
and
25
,
Proposition
28
(b) shows that

\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)
.
By (
D.40
), (
D.39
), and the bound
|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{N,i}|\prec N^{-1/2}
of part (a) of the theorem already proven,
we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z_{N,i},\alpha)}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)}}}\right\|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)-\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z_{N,i},\alpha)}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\prec N^{-1/2}.

(D.42)

Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{v}_{r}
denote the columns of
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}
, which are the unit
eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.
Then, applying
\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in\ker\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{K}}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)
, (
D.42
), and
the definition of
\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z,\alpha)
, and noting that

\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})=z_{N,i}\tilde{m}_{N,0}(z_{N,i})=-z_{N,i}/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{M}_{N}(z_{N,i},\alpha)\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}}}\right\|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+z_{N,i}(1-\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))}}}\right)^{2}(\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i})^{2}\prec 1/N.

For each
j\in[r]\setminus\{i\}
, we have that

z_{N,i}(1-\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))
is bounded away from 0
as
N\to\infty
because
z_{N,i}\to z_{i}>0
and

\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\to\lambda_{j}/\lambda_{i}\neq 1
.
So this implies

|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}_{j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}|^{2}\prec 1/N\quad\text{ for all }\quad j\in[r]\setminus\{i\}.

(D.43)

At the same time, applying Lemma
29

and
|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{N,i}|\prec N^{-1/2}
to
bound (
D.32
) in Proposition
28
(b), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z_{N,i},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{n}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z_{N,i},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\Big((\alpha+z_{N,i})\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}\Big)^{-2}\Big(\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}\Big)\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}_{i}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}|^{2}\cdot\frac{1+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}{\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sum_{j\neq i}|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}_{j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}|^{2}\cdot\frac{1+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}{(\alpha+z_{N,i}(1-\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})))^{2}}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}_{i}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}|^{2}\cdot\frac{1+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}{\alpha^{2}}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right),

(D.44)

the last equality applying (
D.43
).
Observe that

\displaystyle 1+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=1+z_{N,i}\tilde{m}_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})+\tilde{m}_{N,0}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=z_{N,i}\tilde{m}_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=z_{N,i}\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/z_{N,0}^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})),

where the last two equalities use
z_{N,i}=z_{N,0}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))

and
\tilde{m}_{N,0}(\cdot)
is the inverse function of
z_{N,0}(\cdot)
.
Then, multiplying by
\alpha^{2}/(1+\psi_{N,0}^{\prime}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))

we obtain

|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}_{i}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}|^{2}=\frac{z_{N,0}^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))}{z_{N,i}\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)=\varphi_{N,0}({-}1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right),

where we recall
\varphi_{N,0}
from (
B.5
).
We have
\varphi_{N,0}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))\to\varphi(-1/\lambda_{i})=z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{i})/(\lambda_{i}z_{i})>0
, so taking a square root gives

|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}_{i}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}|=\sqrt{\varphi_{N,0}({-}1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right).

(D.45)

Finally, for any unit vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, by (
D.33
) in
Proposition
28
(b),
Lemma
24
(b), and the bound

|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{N,i}|\prec N^{-1/2}
in part (a) of the theorem
already shown, we know that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}=~

\displaystyle{-}\alpha\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}(z_{N,i},\alpha)\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle{-}\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}{\alpha+z_{N,i}+\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle{-}\alpha\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}{\alpha+z_{N,i}\cdot(1-\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right).

Applying (
D.43
) and (
D.45
), only the summand with
j=i

contributes, and we obtain as desired

|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}|=\sqrt{\varphi_{N,0}({-}1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))}\cdot|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}|+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right).

∎

Proof of Theorem
12
(c).

Applying Lemma
25
(b) and block matrix inversion of

\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
to the definitions of

\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}
in (
D.29
) and
(
D.30
), we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}(z,\alpha)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(z+\alpha+\psi_{N,0}(z)\cdot\mathbb{E}[u^{2}]-\psi_{N,0}(z)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]}}\right)^{-1}}}\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}},

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}(z,\alpha)-\frac{\psi_{N,0}(z)\cdot\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}}{z+\alpha+\psi_{N,0}(z)\cdot\mathbb{E}[u^{2}]-\psi_{N,0}(z)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}(z,\alpha)}{\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}(z,\alpha)}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\cdot\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}_{r}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((\alpha+z)\boldsymbol{I}_{r}+\psi_{N,0}(z)\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{r}}}\right)^{-1}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

Applying this and the bound

|\widehat{\lambda}_{i}-z_{N,i}|\prec N^{-1/2}

to Proposition
28
(c),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}=\frac{\alpha}{\widehat{\lambda}_{i}^{1/2}}\frac{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}}_{1V}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)}{\widetilde{}\mathcal{R}_{11}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i},\alpha)}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}_{r}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{z_{N,i}}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})\cdot\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}{\alpha+z_{N,i}+\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{z_{N,i}}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})\cdot\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}{\alpha+z_{N,i}(1-\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right).

Then, applying again (
D.43
) and (
D.45
), only the summand
with
j=i
contributes, and this gives

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}|=~

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathbb{E}[u\boldsymbol{g}]^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}|\cdot|\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})|\sqrt{\varphi_{N,0}(-1/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}))}}{\sqrt{z_{N,i}}}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{-1/2}}}\right).

Recalling
\psi_{N,0}(z_{N,i})=-z_{N,i}/\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
, this yields part
(c) of the theorem.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs for propagation of spiked eigenstructure in deep NNs

We next prove Theorems
2
and
3
.
Appendix
E.1
first
establishes these results for a one-hidden-layer NN,
L=1
. We then apply this
result for
L=1
inductively in Appendix
E.2
to obtain
these results for general
L
. Appendix
E.3
proves
Corollary
4
.

E.1
Spike analysis for one-hidden-layer CK

Consider the setup in Section
2.1
with a single hidden
layer
L=1
. In this setting, let us simplify notation and denote

\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{X}_{0},\qquad\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{W}_{0},\qquad d=d_{0},\qquad N=d_{1},

\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{X}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma(\boldsymbol{W}\boldsymbol{X}),\qquad\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{K}_{1}=\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y}.

We denote the rows of
\boldsymbol{W}
and columns of
\boldsymbol{X}
respectively by

\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ for }i\in[N],\qquad\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ for }\alpha\in[n].

We write
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
for
the expectation over a standard Gaussian vector
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{I})
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Note that for a sufficiently large constant
B>0

(depending on
\mathrm{supp}({\nu})
and
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}
),
Assumption
2
implies that the event

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})=\Big\{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|<B,\;|\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\beta}|<\tau_{n}\text{ and }|\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}\|_{2}-1|<\tau_{n}\text{ for all }\alpha\neq\beta\in[n]\Big\}

(E.1)

holds almost surely for all large
n
. We will use throughout this
section the following argument: Since
\boldsymbol{W}\equiv\boldsymbol{W}^{(n)}
is independent
of
\boldsymbol{X}\equiv\boldsymbol{X}^{(n)}
, and
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X}^{(n)})
holds for all large
n
with
probability 1 over
\{\boldsymbol{X}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, to prove any almost-sure
statement, it suffices to show that the statement holds with
probability 1 over
\{\boldsymbol{W}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, for any deterministic
matrices
\{\boldsymbol{X}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfying
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X}^{(n)})
.
Thus, we assume in the remainder of this section that

\boldsymbol{X}
is deterministic and satisfies
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})

for all large
n
, and write
\mathbb{E},\mathbb{P}
for the
expectation and probability over only the random weight matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
.

We will apply Theorem
12
to a centered version of
\boldsymbol{Y}
,

\boldsymbol{G}:=\boldsymbol{Y}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}[\boldsymbol{g}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}_{N}]^{\top},\qquad\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}:=\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}})-\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}})].

Note that these rows
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}
are i.i.d. with mean
\mathbf{0}
and covariance

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}:=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\top}\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]-\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}.

(E.2)

Lemma 30
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and

3
hold, with
L=1
and deterministic
\boldsymbol{X}
. Then

\|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]\|_{2}\to 0,\qquad\|\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Y}\|\to 0.

Proof.

Denote
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Applying
\mathbb{E}[\sigma(\xi)]=0
,

\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime}(\xi)\xi]=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\xi)]=0
, and a
Taylor approximation of
\sigma
, for any
\alpha\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha})]=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sigma(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}}}}\right\|\xi)]-\mathbb{E}[\sigma(\xi)]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime}(\xi)\xi(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}}}}\right\|-1)]+\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\xi^{2}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}}}}\right\|-1)^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\xi^{2}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}}}}\right\|-1)^{2}]

for some
\eta
between
\xi
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}_{i}}}}\right\|\xi
.
Then, applying
|\sigma^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq\lambda_{\sigma}
and the

\tau_{n}
-orthonormality of
\boldsymbol{X}
under
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})
,

|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha})]|\leq\lambda_{\sigma}\tau_{n}^{2}.

This gives
\|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]\|_{2}\leq\lambda_{\sigma}\tau_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}\to 0
,
so also

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Y}}}}\right\|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathbf{1}_{N}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]}}}\right\|\to 0
.
∎

Next, we recall from
[
WZ21
]

an approximation of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
by the linearized matrix

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=b_{\sigma}^{2}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}+(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})\boldsymbol{I}_{n}

(E.3)

in the operator norm.

Lemma 31
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and

3
hold, with
L=1
and deterministic
\boldsymbol{X}
.

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}\|\to 0.

Consequently, ordering
\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})

in the same order as

\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})
,

\sup_{i\in[n]}\Big|b_{\sigma}^{2}\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})+(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})-\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\Big|\to 0.

(E.4)

Proof.

Denote
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Let
\zeta_{k}(\sigma)=\mathbb{E}[\sigma(\xi)h_{k}(\xi)]
be
the
k
-th Hermite coefficient of
\sigma
, where
h_{k}(x)
is the
k
-th
Hermite polynomial normalized so that
\mathbb{E}[h_{k}(\xi)^{2}]=1
. Note that by Gaussian
integration by parts and the assumption
\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\xi)]=0
,

\zeta_{1}(\sigma)=\mathbb{E}[\xi\sigma(\xi)]=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime}(\xi)]=b_{\sigma},\qquad\sqrt{2}\,\zeta_{2}(\sigma)=\mathbb{E}[(\xi^{2}-1)\sigma(\xi)]=\mathbb{E}[\xi\sigma^{\prime}(\xi)]=\mathbb{E}[\sigma^{\prime\prime}(\xi)]=0.

Then by
[
WZ21
, Lemma 5.2]
and the first statement
of Lemma
30
, we have

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|\leq\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}-\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\top}\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]\|+\|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\top}\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})]-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|\to 0

where

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}=\zeta_{1}(\sigma)^{2}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}+\zeta_{3}(\sigma)^{2}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\odot 3}+(1-\zeta_{1}(\sigma)^{2}-\zeta_{3}(\sigma)^{2})\boldsymbol{I}_{n}.

(Here, examination of the proof of
[
WZ21
, Lemma 5.2]
shows
that the condition
\sum_{\alpha}(\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}\|_{2}-1)^{2}\leq B^{2}
for

(\varepsilon,B)
-orthonormality is not
used when
\zeta_{2}(\sigma)=0
, and the remaining conditions of

(\varepsilon,B)
-orthonormality hold under
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})
.)
The lemma then follows upon observing that under
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})
,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\odot 3}-\boldsymbol{I}_{n}}}}\right\|\leq~

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diag}((\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\odot 3}-\boldsymbol{I}_{n})}}}\right\|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{offdiag}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\odot 3}}}}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\max_{\alpha\in[n]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}}}}\right\|^{6}-1}}\right|+n\cdot\max_{\alpha\neq\beta\in[n]}|\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\beta}|^{3}\leq C(\tau_{n}+n\tau_{n}^{3}),

so that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{0}}}}\right\|=\zeta_{3}(\sigma)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\odot 3}-\boldsymbol{I}_{n}}}}\right\|\to 0
when
\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}\cdot n^{1/3}=0
.
∎

Theorem
12
will provide a characterization
of outlier eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{K}
that are separated from

\mathcal{S}_{1}=\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})\cup\{0\}
, which is different from
\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})
when

\gamma_{1}<1
. For
\gamma_{1}<1
, we augment this
statement with a small-ball argument to bound the smallest eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{K}
,
using the following result of
[
Yas16
, Theorem 2.1]
.

Lemma 32

(
[
Yas16
]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{G}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}[\boldsymbol{g}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}_{N}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times n}
where
the rows
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are i.i.d. and equal in law to
\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Define

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{g}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top},\qquad c_{\boldsymbol{g}}=\inf_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}=1}\mathbb{E}|\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|,\qquad L_{\boldsymbol{g}}(\delta,\iota)=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\Pi}:\mathrm{rank}({\boldsymbol{\Pi}})\geq\iota n}\mathbb{P}\Big[|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{g}|^{2}\leq\delta\,\mathrm{rank}({\boldsymbol{\Pi}})\Big]

where the latter supremum is taken over all orthogonal projections

\boldsymbol{\Pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with rank at least
\iota\cdot n
.

Suppose
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\leq 1
,
c(\boldsymbol{g})\geq c
, and
n/N\leq y
for some constants
c>0
and
y\in(0,1)
. Then there exist constants
s_{0},\iota>0

depending only on
(c,y)
such that for any
\delta\in(0,1)
and
s>0
,

\mathbb{P}[\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G})\geq(s_{0}-L(\delta,\iota;\boldsymbol{g})-s)\delta]\geq 1-2e^{-yns^{2}/2}.

Lemma 33
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
, and

3
hold, with
L=1
and deterministic
\boldsymbol{X}
. Let

\boldsymbol{G}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Y}
.

(a)

If
\gamma_{1}\geq 1
, then
0\in\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})
.

(b)

If
\gamma_{1}<1
, then there is a constant
c>0
such that

\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G})>c
almost surely for all large
n
.

Proof.

If
\gamma_{1}>1
strictly, then by definition

\mu_{1}=\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\,\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\frac{\gamma_{1}-1}{\gamma_{1}}\,\delta_{0}

is a mixture of
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
and a point mass at 0, so
0\in\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})
.
If
\gamma=1
, then
\mu_{1}=\tilde{\mu}_{1}
. In this case,
recall from Proposition
8

that
\mathrm{supp}({\tilde{\mu}_{1}})
is characterized by the function

z(\tilde{m})={-}\frac{1}{\tilde{m}}+\gamma_{1}\int\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda\tilde{m}}d\nu_{0}(\lambda).

When
\gamma_{1}=1
, we have for all
\tilde{m}\in(0,\infty)
that

z(\tilde{m})<0,\qquad z^{\prime}(\tilde{m})=\frac{1}{\tilde{m}^{2}}-\int\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(1+\lambda\tilde{m})^{2}}d\nu(\lambda)>0,

so
z(\tilde{m})
increases from
-\infty
to 0 over the positive line

\tilde{m}\in(0,\infty)
. Suppose by contradiction that

0\notin\mathrm{supp}({\tilde{\mu}})
. Then by
Proposition
8
, there must be a point
\tilde{m}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}
where
z(\tilde{m})=0
and
z^{\prime}(\tilde{m})>0
strictly, implying that
there is an open interval
(\tilde{m}_{-},\tilde{m}_{+})\ni\tilde{m}

on which
z(\cdot)
increases from
z(\tilde{m}_{-})<0
to
z(\tilde{m}_{+})>0
.
We must have
\tilde{m}<0
by the above behavior of
z(\cdot)
on

(0,\infty)
, and the range
[z(\tilde{m}_{-}),z(\tilde{m}_{+})]
must overlap with

[z(a),z(b)]
for some sufficiently large
a,b\in(0,\infty)
.
But this contradicts the non-intersecting property
shown in
[
SC95
, Theorem 4.4]
. So
also in this case
0\in\mathrm{supp}({\tilde{\mu}_{1}})=\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})
, showing part (a).

For part (b), we apply Lemma
32
. Under
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})
,
the condition
b_{\sigma}\neq 0
implies

c_{0}<\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}})<C_{0}
for some constants
C_{0},c_{0}>0
.
Hence also

c_{0}<\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})<C_{0}

(E.5)

for all large
n
, by Lemma
31
.
We may assume without loss of generality that

\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\leq 1
as needed in Lemma
32
;
otherwise, the following argument may be applied to a rescaling of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

and
\boldsymbol{G}
.

To lower bound
c_{\boldsymbol{g}}
in Lemma
32
, observe that
for any unit vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have

\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v})^{2}]=\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{v}>c_{0}.

Viewing
F(\boldsymbol{w})=\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}=\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{v}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}v_{\alpha}\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha})
as a function of
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{I})
, we have

\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}v_{\alpha}\sigma^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha})\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{v}\odot\sigma^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}))
where

\sigma^{\prime}(\cdot)
is applied coordinatewise and
\odot
is the coordinatewise
product. Then, applying
|\sigma^{\prime}(x)|\leq\lambda_{\sigma}
, observe that

\|\nabla F(\boldsymbol{w})\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{X}\|\cdot\|\boldsymbol{v}\odot\sigma^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})\|_{2}\leq\lambda_{\sigma}\|\boldsymbol{X}\|
, so
F(\boldsymbol{w})
is
C
-Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{w}

for a constant
C>0
(not depending on
\boldsymbol{v}
) on the event
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})
.
This implies by Gaussian
concentration-of-measure that
\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}
is sub-gaussian,
i.e. for some constants
C,c>0
and any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}[|\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|\geq t]\leq Ce^{-ct^{2}}
.
Integrating this tail bound, for some constant
t>0
sufficiently large,
we have
\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v})^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{|\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|>t\}}]\leq c_{0}/2
, and hence

c_{0}<\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v})^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v})^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{|\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|\leq t\}}]+\frac{c_{0}}{2}\leq t\cdot\mathbb{E}|\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|+\frac{c_{0}}{2}.

So
\mathbb{E}|\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|\geq c_{0}/(2t)
, and hence
c_{\boldsymbol{g}}\geq c_{0}/(2t)>c
for a
constant
c>0
.

Now let
s_{0},\iota>0
be the constants depending on
(c,\gamma_{1})
in the
statement of Lemma
32
.
By the nonlinear Hanson-Wright inequality of

[
WZ21
, Eq. (3.3)]
, for any orthogonal projection
\boldsymbol{\Pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, any
t>0
, and some constant
c>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big[|\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{g}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{g}|>t\big]\leq 2e^{-c\min(t^{2}/\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\|_{F}^{2},t/\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\|)}.

Here
\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{g}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{g}=\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}>c_{0}\mathrm{rank}({\boldsymbol{\Pi}})
,

\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\|_{F}^{2}=\mathrm{rank}({\boldsymbol{\Pi}})
, and
\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\|=1
,
so applying this with
t=(c_{0}/2)\mathrm{rank}({\boldsymbol{\Pi}})
yields

\mathbb{P}[|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{g}|^{2}\leq(c_{0}/2)\mathrm{rank}({\boldsymbol{\Pi}})]\leq 2e^{-c^{\prime}\mathrm{rank}({\boldsymbol{\Pi}})}.

Then, choosing
\delta=c_{0}/2
, we get
L_{\boldsymbol{g}}(\delta,\iota)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then Lemma
32
implies
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G})>s_{0}\delta/2
almost surely for all large
n
, as desired.
∎

The following is the main result of this section, showing that Theorems

2
and
3
hold in this setting of
L=1
.

Lemma 34
.

Theorems
2
and

3
hold for a single layer
L=1
.
Furthermore,
\boldsymbol{Y}
is
C\tau_{n}
-orthonormal for some constant
C>0
,
almost surely for all large
n
.

Proof.

We condition on
\boldsymbol{X}
as discussed at the start of this section, and
apply Theorem
12
to the centered matrix

\boldsymbol{G}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}[\boldsymbol{g}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}_{N}]^{\top}
.
Let us verify Assumption
5
for
\boldsymbol{G}
:
We have shown Assumption
5
(a) in (
E.5
).
The rows
\boldsymbol{g}_{i}
are sub-gaussian as shown in the above proof of
Lemma
33
(b), so Assumption
5
(b) holds by

[
Ver10
, Eq. (5.26)]
, and
Assumption
5
(d) holds by
[
JNG+19
, Lemma 2]
.
The nonlinear Hanson-Wright inequality of

[
WZ21
, Eq. (3.3)]
implies

|\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|\prec\|\boldsymbol{A}\|_{F}

uniformly over
i\in[N]
and deterministic matrices

\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
. Furthermore, it is clear from the argument preceding

[
WZ21
, Eq. (3.3)]
that for any
i\neq j\in[N]
, the joint
vector
(\boldsymbol{g}_{i},\boldsymbol{g}_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
also satisfies Lipschitz concentration, hence

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}^{\top}&\boldsymbol{g}_{j}^{\top}\end{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{B}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{g}_{i}\\
\boldsymbol{g}_{j}\end{pmatrix}-\operatorname{Tr}\boldsymbol{B}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\end{pmatrix}}}\right|\prec\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}

uniformly over
i\neq j\in[N]
and deterministic matrices
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{2n\times 2n}
. Applying this with

\boldsymbol{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\boldsymbol{A}\\
\boldsymbol{A}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}

verifies both statements of Assumption
5
(c).

Next, we check Assumption
6
for the population
covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.
Combining Assumption
2
and (
E.4
) from
Lemma
31
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-r}\sum_{i=r+1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}

\displaystyle\to\nu_{0}:=b_{\sigma}^{2}\otimes\mu_{0}\oplus(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})\text{ weakly},

(E.6)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\to-\frac{1}{s_{i,0}}:=b_{\sigma}^{2}\lambda_{i}+(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})\not\in\mathrm{supp}({\nu_{0}})\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,r.

(E.7)

Here, the statement
-1/s_{i,0}\notin\mathrm{supp}({\nu_{0}})
in (
E.7
)
follows from the assumptions

\lambda_{i}\notin\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{0}})
and
b_{\sigma}\neq 0
.
This then implies by the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{1}

that
s_{i,0}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{1}
, as claimed in Theorem
3
(a).
Furthermore, for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
and all large
n
,
Assumption
2
and (
E.4
) imply also that

\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\in\mathrm{supp}({\nu_{0}})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\text{ for all }i\geq r+1.

Thus Assumption
6
holds for
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
as
n\to\infty
.

Then we can apply Theorems
10
and
12

for
\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}:=\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}
. The Stieltjes transform approximation in Theorem

10
and Lemma
23
(c) together
imply
m_{\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}}(z)\to m_{1}(z)
almost surely for each fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,
where
m_{1}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of the measure

\mu_{1}=\rho_{\gamma_{1}}^{\textnormal{MP}}\boxtimes\nu_{0}
. This implies the weak convergence

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})}\to\mu_{1}\text{ a.s.}

(E.8)

Theorem
12
(a,b) further justifies:

•

Let
z_{1}(\cdot)
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
be defined by (
2.7
) and
(
2.8
) with
\ell=1
. Then for any sufficiently small
constant
\varepsilon>0
, almost surely for all large
n
,
there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between the eigenvalues of
\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}
outside

\mathcal{S}_{1}+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
\{i:i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\}
. Furthermore,
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
,

\lambda_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})\to z_{1}(s_{i,0})>0.

(E.9)

almost surely as
n\to\infty
.

•

Let
\varphi_{1}(\cdot)
be defined by (
2.7
) with
\ell=1
.
For each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
, let
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

be a unit-norm eigenvector of
\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}

corresponding to
\lambda_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})
, and for each
j\in[r]
,
let
\boldsymbol{v}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
be a unit-norm eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

corresponding to
\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
. Then
almost surely as
n\to\infty
, for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
j\in[r]
,

|\boldsymbol{v}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})|\to\sqrt{\varphi_{1}(s_{i,0})}\cdot\mathbf{1}\{i=j\}

(E.10)

where
\varphi_{1}(s_{i,0})>0
. Moreover,
letting
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be any unit vector independent of
\boldsymbol{W}
, almost surely

|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})|-\sqrt{\varphi_{1}(s_{i,0})}\cdot|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})|\to 0.

(E.11)

If
\gamma_{1}\geq 1
, then Lemma
33
(a) shows that

\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})=\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})\cup\{0\}=\mathcal{S}_{1}
. If
\gamma_{1}<1
, then
Lemma
33
(b) shows that for any sufficiently small constant

\varepsilon>0
,
\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}
has no eigenvalues in
[0,\varepsilon)
almost surely
for all large
n
. Thus, in both cases, the first statement above in fact
establishes a 1-to-1 correspondence between
\{i:i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\}
and
all eigenvalues of
\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}
outside
\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, almost surely
for all large
n
.

To translate these statements to the non-centered matrix

\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y}
, recall from Lemma
30
that
\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{G}\|\to 0
almost surely, and from Assumption
5
(b) verified above that

\mathbf{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}\boldsymbol{G}\|>B^{\prime}\}\prec 0
for a constant
B^{\prime}>0
.
Then, almost surely as
n\to\infty
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{K}-\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}}}}\right\|\to 0.

Therefore, by Weyl’s inequality and (
E.8
), the
empirical eigenvalue distribution
\widehat{\mu}_{1}
of
\boldsymbol{K}
converges also to

\mu_{1}
weakly a.s., as claimed in Theorem
2
.
Furthermore, by (
E.9
), almost surely for all
large
n
, the eigenvalues
\widehat{\lambda}_{i,1}
of
\boldsymbol{K}
outside

\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
are also in 1-to-1
correspondence with
\{i:i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\}
, where

\widehat{\lambda}_{i,1}\to z_{1}(s_{i,0})
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
. In particular,
if
r=0
, then also
|\mathcal{I}_{1}|=0
, so
\boldsymbol{K}
has no eigenvalues outside

\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
. This proves Theorem
2
.

For each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
, let
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
unit-norm eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{K}

corresponding to
\widehat{\lambda}_{i,1}
.
Then by the Davis-Kahan Theorem
[
DK70
]
,
we may choose a sign for
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,1}
such that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})}}}\right\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}\|\boldsymbol{K}-\bar{\boldsymbol{K}}\|}{\textnormal{dist}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i,1},\operatorname{spec}{(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})}\setminus\{\lambda_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})\})}.

We note that
\widehat{\lambda}_{i,1}\to z_{1}(s_{i,0})
a.s., which is distinct
from the limit values
\{z_{1}(s_{j,0}):j\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\setminus\{i\}\}
of

\{\lambda_{j}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}):\mathcal{I}_{1}\setminus\{i\}\}

by bijectivity of the map
z_{1}(\cdot)
in Proposition

8
. Furthermore
z_{1}(s_{i,0})
falls outside

\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{1}})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,
which contains all other eigenvalues of
\bar{}\boldsymbol{K}
.
Thus
\textnormal{dist}(\widehat{\lambda}_{i,1},\operatorname{spec}{(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})}\setminus\{\lambda_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})\})\geq c
for a constant
c>0
almost surely for
all large
n
, so

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{i}(\bar{}\boldsymbol{K})}}}\right\|\to 0\text{ a.s.}

Similarly, by the convergence

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}\|\to 0
and the assumption
b_{\sigma}\neq 0
,
we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})-\boldsymbol{v}_{j}}}}\right\|\to 0
for each
j\in[r]
, where
\boldsymbol{v}_{j}

is the unit-norm eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}
corresponding to
its eigenvalue

b_{\sigma}^{2}\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})+(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})
, i.e. the
eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}
corresponding to
\lambda_{j}(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})
.
Then (
E.10
) and (
E.11
) imply also

|\boldsymbol{v}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}|^{2}\to\varphi_{1}(s_{i,0})\cdot\mathbf{1}\{i=j\},\qquad|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}|^{2}-\varphi_{1}(s_{i,0})\cdot|\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}|^{2}\to 0.

This shows all claims of Theorem
3
for
L=1
.

Finally, on
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})
, the matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}
is
C\tau_{n}
-orthonormal for a
constant
C>0
by
[
FW20
, Lemma D.3(b)]
.
(The proof of
[
FW20
, Lemma D.3(b)]
again does not use
the condition
\sum_{\alpha}(\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}\|_{2}^{2}-1)^{2}\leq B^{2}
of

(\varepsilon,B)
-orthonormality therein, and the remaining conditions of

(\varepsilon,B)
-orthonormality hold under
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{X})
.)
This shows the last claim of the lemma.
∎

E.2
Spike analysis for multiple layers

We now prove Theorem
3
by inductively applying the result for

L=1
through multiple layers. We follow the notations of
Section
2.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Suppose inductively that Assumption
2

holds with
\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell-1}
in place of
\boldsymbol{X}_{0}
, and all
conclusions of Theorem
3
hold for
\boldsymbol{K}_{\ell}
. The base case of

\ell=1
follows from Lemma
34
.

Then the last statement of Lemma
34
implies that

\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}
is
\tau_{n}^{\prime}
-orthonormal almost surely for all large
n
,
for some
\tau_{n}^{\prime}
satisfying
\tau_{n}^{\prime}\cdot n^{1/3}\to 0
.
Furthermore, the conclusions of Theorem
3
(b,c) for
\boldsymbol{K}_{\ell}

imply that statements (a) and (b) of Assumption
2
also
hold for
\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}
, in the following sense: Let
r_{\ell}=|\mathcal{I}_{\ell}|
. Then

\frac{1}{n-|r_{\ell}|}\sum_{i\notin\mathcal{I}_{\ell}}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell})}\to\mu_{\ell}\text{ weakly a.s.}

For any fixed
\varepsilon>0
, almost surely for all large
n
,

\widehat{\lambda}_{i,\ell}:=\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell})\in\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{\ell}})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)

for all
i\notin\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
. Furthermore, for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
,

\widehat{\lambda}_{i,\ell}\to z_{\ell}(s_{i,\ell-1})\notin\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{\ell}})
.

Then we may apply Lemma
34
with input data

\boldsymbol{X}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}
in place of
\boldsymbol{X}_{0}
. This shows that for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
and all large
n
, there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between the eigenvalues
\widehat{\lambda}_{i,\ell+1}
of
\boldsymbol{K}_{\ell+1}
outside

\mathrm{supp}({\mu_{\ell+1}})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
\{i:i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell+1}\}
,
where

\widehat{\lambda}_{i,\ell+1}\to z_{\ell+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s_{i,\ell}}}\right)>0
a.s.,
and
s_{i,\ell}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1}
.
Moreover, for any unit vector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
independent of

\boldsymbol{W}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{W}_{\ell+1}
,

|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,\ell+1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}-\varphi_{\ell+1}(s_{i,\ell})\cdot|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,\ell}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\to 0,

where also
\varphi_{\ell+1}(s_{i,\ell})>0
.
Then by the induction hypothesis for
|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,\ell}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,\ell+1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\to~

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\varphi_{k}(s_{i,k-1})\cdot|\boldsymbol{v}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}|^{2},

and specializing to
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}_{j}
for
j\in[r]
gives

|\widehat{}\boldsymbol{v}_{i,\ell+1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}|^{2}\to\prod_{k=1}^{\ell+1}\varphi_{k}(s_{i,k-1})\cdot\mathbf{1}\{i=j\}.

This verifies all conclusions of Theorem
3

for
\boldsymbol{K}_{\ell+1}
, completing the induction.
∎

E.3
Corollary for signal-plus-noise input data

Proof of Corollary
4
.

It is shown in
[
BGN12
, Section 3.1]
that asymptotically as

d,n\to\infty
with
n/d\to\gamma_{0}
, the data matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
has a spike
singular value corresponding to
\theta_{i}
if and only if

\theta_{i}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
, in which case

\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{K}_{0})\to\lambda_{i}:=\frac{(1+\theta_{i}^{2})(\gamma_{0}+\theta_{i}^{2})}{\theta_{i}^{2}},\qquad|\boldsymbol{b}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}_{i}|^{2}\to 1-\frac{\gamma_{0}(1+\theta_{i}^{2})}{\theta_{i}^{2}(\theta_{i}^{2}+\gamma_{0})}

where
\boldsymbol{v}_{i}
is the unit eigenvector of the input Gram matrix

\boldsymbol{K}_{0}=\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}
. Thus claims (a) and (b) of Assumption
2

hold with
r=|\{i:\theta_{i}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}\}|
,

\mu_{0}=\rho_{\gamma_{0}}^{\textnormal{MP}}
being the standard Marcenko-Pastur law, and

\lambda_{i}=(1+\theta_{i}^{2})(\gamma_{0}+\theta_{i}^{2})/\theta_{i}^{2}

being the above values.

We note that
\boldsymbol{X}
is
n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}
-orthonormal for any
\varepsilon>0

almost surely for all large
n
, by the given condition

\max_{i=1}^{r}\|\boldsymbol{b}_{i}\|_{\infty}<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}
and the bounds, for any

\alpha,\beta\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\alpha}}}\right\|_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\|_{2}|\theta_{i}||b_{i,\alpha}|}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\alpha}}}\right\|_{2}+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}}}\right)=1+O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}}}\right),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{\alpha}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{z}_{\alpha}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{\beta}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\theta_{i}|\Big(|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{\alpha}||b_{i,\alpha}|+|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{\beta}||b_{i,\beta}|\Big)+\theta_{i}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}|b_{i,\alpha}b_{i,\beta}|}}\right)=O_{\prec}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}}}\right).

Hence Theorem
3
applies, showing
that
\boldsymbol{K}_{\ell}
has an outlier eigenvalue corresponding to each input
signal
\theta_{i}
if and only if
\theta_{i}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
and

i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
. The statement (
2.10
) follows from
Theorem
3
(c) applied with
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{b}_{i}
.
∎

Appendix F
Proofs for spiked eigenstructure of the trained CK

In this section, we prove Theorem
5
. The proof is an
application of Theorem
12
as in the one-layer setting of the
preceding section, but now reversing the roles of
\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{W}
. We abbreviate

\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{W}_{\text{trained}},\qquad\boldsymbol{Y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma(\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}\boldsymbol{W}),\qquad\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{Y}\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}

where
\boldsymbol{K}
is the CK matrix of interest. In contrast to the preceding section,
the theorem requires characterizing the
left
spike singular vector of

\boldsymbol{Y}
, and we will do so using Theorem
12
(c).

We first recall the following approximation of
\boldsymbol{W}
from
[
BES+22
]
.

Proposition 35
.

Under Assumption
4
, set
\eta=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t}
, and let

\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
be as defined in (
2.14
). Then

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{W}-\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{W}\|\prec N^{-1/2}\quad\text{ where }\quad\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{W}=\boldsymbol{W}_{0}+\frac{b_{\sigma}\eta}{n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}.

(F.1)

The largest singular value
s_{\max}(\boldsymbol{W})
falls outside the limit of its
empirical singular value distribution if and only if

\theta_{1}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
, in which case
s_{\max}(\boldsymbol{W})

and its unit-norm left singular vector
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{W})
satisfy

s_{\max}(\boldsymbol{W})\to s_{1}:=\sqrt{\frac{(1+\theta_{1}^{2})(\gamma_{0}+\theta_{1}^{2})}{\theta_{1}^{2}}},\quad|\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{W})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}|^{2}\to\frac{\theta_{2}^{2}}{\theta_{1}^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\gamma_{0}+\theta_{1}^{2}}{\theta_{1}^{2}(\theta_{1}^{2}+1)}}}\right)\quad\text{a.s.}

(F.2)

Proof.

Notice that each gradient update matrix

\boldsymbol{G}_{t}
of (
2.12
) takes the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{G}_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{y}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{W}_{\!t})\boldsymbol{a}}}\right)\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}}}\right)\odot\sigma^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{W}_{\!t})}}\right],

From the proof of
[
BES+22
, Lemma 16]
, for each
t=1,\ldots,T
, this
matrix
\boldsymbol{G}_{t}
satisfies the same rank-one approximation

\Big\|\sqrt{N}\,\boldsymbol{G}_{t}-\frac{b_{\sigma}}{n}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\Big\|\prec N^{-1/2}.

This implies (
F.1
) in light of (
2.12
),
and the statements of (
F.2
) then
follow from
[
BES+22
, Theorem 3]
.
∎

We denote the columns of
\boldsymbol{W}\equiv\boldsymbol{W}_{\text{trained}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}

and of the initialization
\boldsymbol{W}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}
by

\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\;\boldsymbol{w}_{i,0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ for }i\in[N]

respectively. Fixing a large constant
B>0
and small constant
\varepsilon>0
,
define the event

\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W})=\Big\{\|\boldsymbol{W}\|<B,\,|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{j}|<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}\text{ and }|\|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\|_{2}-1|<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}\text{ for all }i\neq j\in[N]\Big\}.

Lemma 36
.

Under Assumption
4
, for some sufficiently large constant

B>0
and any fixed
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W})
holds almost surely for all large
n
and any fixed
T\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By the assumption
[\boldsymbol{W}_{0}]_{ij}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)
, it is
immediate to check that
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W}_{0})
holds almost surely for all large
n
.
To show that
