\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W})
holds, we apply the approximation (
F.1
).
Here, under Assumption
4
,
we have by standard tail bounds for Gaussian vectors and matrices that

\mathbf{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y}\|>Cn\}\leq\mathbf{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{X}\|\cdot(\lambda_{\sigma_{*}}\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\|_{2})>Cn\}\prec 0,\qquad\mathbf{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}>C\}\prec 0

for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
, and also

\|\boldsymbol{a}\|_{\infty}\prec N^{-1/2}
. Then this implies

\displaystyle\max_{i=1}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i,0}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i=1}^{N}\|\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i,0}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{W}-\widetilde{}\boldsymbol{W}\|\prec N^{-1/2}

(F.3)

and
\mathbf{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{W}-\boldsymbol{W}_{0}\|>C^{\prime}\}\prec 0
for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
. Then
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W})

also holds almost surely for all large
n
, as claimed.
∎

Analogous to the argument of Appendix
E.1
,
we may now condition on
\boldsymbol{W}
, i.e. we assume that
\boldsymbol{W}
is deterministic and
satisfies
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W})
for all large
n
, and we
write
\mathbb{E}
for the expectation
over only the randomness of the new data
(\tilde{}\boldsymbol{X},\tilde{}\boldsymbol{y})
.
Defining

\boldsymbol{G}=\sqrt{\frac{N}{n}}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Y})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times N},\quad\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\tilde{}\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\quad\text{ where }\quad\boldsymbol{Y}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sigma(\tilde{}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{W}),

(F.4)

observe that
[\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{G}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(N+1)}
has centered i.i.d. rows with
respect to the randomness of
(\tilde{}\boldsymbol{X},\tilde{}\boldsymbol{y})
.
We will write
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
for the expectation with respect to a standard Gaussian
vector
\boldsymbol{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{d})
.

Lemma 37
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{W}
satisfies
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W})
for all large
n
. Then

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})]\|_{2}\to 0,\qquad\|\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{Y}\|\to 0,\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}}}}\right\|\to 0

(F.5)

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}:=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})^{\top}\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})]-\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})]^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})]

(F.6)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=~

\displaystyle b_{\sigma}^{2}(\boldsymbol{W}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})+(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})\boldsymbol{I}_{N}.

(F.7)

Proof.

The proof is the same as Lemmas
30
and
31
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5
.

We condition on
\boldsymbol{W}
satisfying
\mathcal{E}(\boldsymbol{W})
for all large
n
, and we
apply Theorem
12
(c) for
[\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{G}]\in\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)\times N}

(exchanging
n
and
N
). It may be checked that
Assumption
5
holds for
[\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{G}]
by the same argument as
in Lemma
34
.

By the convergence
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{lin}}\|\to 0
in
Lemma
37
and Proposition
35
,
if
\theta_{1}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
, then
Assumption
6
holds for
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
with
r=1
and

\nu=b_{\sigma}^{2}\otimes\rho^{\textnormal{MP}}_{\gamma_{0}}\oplus(1-b_{\sigma}^{2}),\qquad\lambda_{1}=b_{\sigma}^{2}\frac{(1+\theta_{1}^{2})(\gamma_{0}+\theta_{1}^{2})}{\theta_{1}^{2}}+(1-b_{\sigma}^{2})\notin\mathrm{supp}({\nu}),

where
\rho_{\gamma_{0}}^{\textnormal{MP}}
is the standard Marcenko-Pastur limit
for the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\boldsymbol{W}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}
, hence
\nu
is
the limit empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, and
\lambda_{1}
is the
limit of
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
. If instead
\theta_{1}\leq\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
,
then Assumption
6
holds with
r=0
.

Then Theorem
12
(a,c)
characterizes the outlier eigenvalue and eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{G}\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}
, showing:

•

\boldsymbol{G}\boldsymbol{G}^{\top}
has a spike eigenvalue if and only if

\theta_{1}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
and
z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{1})>0
, where
z(\cdot)
is defined
by (
A.4
) with
\gamma=\gamma_{1}
and the measure
\nu
given above.
In this case,
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{G}\boldsymbol{G}^{\top})\to z(-1/\lambda_{1})
almost surely.

•

When
\theta_{1}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
and
z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{1})>0
,
letting
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{G}),\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
be the leading unit-norm left singular vector
of
\boldsymbol{G}
and leading unit-norm eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, almost surely

|\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{G})|-\frac{\sqrt{z(-1/\lambda_{1})\varphi({-}1/\lambda_{1})}}{\lambda_{1}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma_{*}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x})\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})]\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}}\right|\to 0.

where
\varphi(\cdot)
is defined by (
B.6
) also
with
\gamma=\gamma_{1}
and the above measure
\nu
.

By an application of Weyl’s inequality and the Davis-Kahan Theorem as in the proof of
Theorem
3
, this implies for
\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{Y}\boldsymbol{Y}^{\top}
that if

\theta_{1}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
and
z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{1})>0
, then its leading
eigenvalue
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{K})
and unit eigenvector
\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}
satisfy

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{K})\to\gamma_{1}^{-1}z(-1/\lambda_{1}),

(F.8)

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\widehat{}\boldsymbol{u}|-\frac{\sqrt{z(-1/\lambda_{1})\varphi({-}1/\lambda_{1})}}{\lambda_{1}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma_{*}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x})\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})]\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{W})}}\right|\to 0,

where
\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{W})
is the leading unit eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\text{lin}}
,
i.e. the leading right singular vector of
\boldsymbol{W}
.
If
\theta_{1}\leq\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
or
z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{1})\leq 0
, then all
eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{K}
converge to the support of its limiting empirical
eigenvalue law.

Finally, in the case of
\theta_{1}>\gamma_{0}^{1/4}
and
z^{\prime}(-1/\lambda_{1})>0
,
we may conclude the proof by showing

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma_{*}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x})\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W})]-b_{\sigma}b_{\sigma_{*}}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}}}\right\|_{2}\to 0\text{ a.s.}

(F.9)

For each column
i\in[N]
, we have from (
F.3
) that

\|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}-\boldsymbol{w}_{i,0}\|_{2}\prec N^{-1/2}
, where
\boldsymbol{w}_{i,0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,d^{-1}\boldsymbol{I})

and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}
is deterministic. Hence
(\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*})
satisfy the approximate
orthonormality conditions
|\|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\|_{2}-1|\prec N^{-1/2}
,
\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}\|_{2}-1=0
,
and
|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}|\prec N^{-1/2}
. Then

[
FW20
, Lemma D.3(a)]
implies

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\sigma_{*}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x})\sigma(\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{i})]-b_{\sigma}b_{\sigma_{*}}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}}}\right|\prec N^{-1}.

(We note that
[
FW20
, Lemma D.3(a)]
assumes
\sigma=\sigma_{*}
, but the proof is
identical for
\sigma\neq\sigma_{*}
both satisfying
Assumption
3
.)
Applying this to each coordinate
i\in[N]
yields (
F.9
).
Observe that
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}\boldsymbol{v}(\boldsymbol{W})=s_{\max}(\boldsymbol{W})\cdot\boldsymbol{\beta}_{*}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{W})

where
s_{\max}(\boldsymbol{W})
and
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{W})
are the leading singular value and left
singular vector of
\boldsymbol{W}
, and recall from the definitions (
F.4
)
that
\boldsymbol{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\tilde{}\boldsymbol{y}
.
Then we can apply (
F.9
) and
Proposition
35
to (
F.8
) to conclude that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}|\tilde{}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}|\to b_{\sigma}b_{\sigma_{*}}\frac{\sqrt{z(-1/\lambda_{1})\varphi(-1/\lambda_{1})}}{\lambda_{1}}\cdot\frac{\theta_{2}\sqrt{(\theta_{1}^{4}-\gamma_{0})(\gamma_{0}+\theta_{1}^{2})}}{\theta_{1}^{3}}>0\text{ a.s.}

∎
